IQM Star 24: L4 `∅G-bus` (Atlas gap — Star topology: central computational resonator bus giving all-to-all within the cell (degr); VLQ: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; VLQ: L4 `∅G-bus` (Atlas gap — star topology: 24 qubits connected to one central resonator, all-to-all through it); KRISS 50-qubit-class superconducting system: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; OQC Toshiko: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; QuamCore: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; Rigetti Cepheus-1-108Q: L10 `∅G-calib` (Atlas gap — ABAA (alternating-bias assisted annealing) post-fab JJ frequency targeting, 97.4% success ); Rigetti Cepheus-1-36Q: L10 `∅G-calib` (Atlas gap — ABAA (alternating-bias assisted annealing) post-fab JJ frequency targeting, 97.4% success ); RIKEN 64-qubit superconducting processor: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; RIKEN-Fujitsu 256-qubit superconducting quantum computer: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; SEEQC SFQ-controlled 5-qubit system: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; Zuchongzhi 3.2: L6 `∅G-lru` (Atlas gap — all-microwave leakage-reduction units on data qubits; leakage population suppressed 72× to).

#### 8.3.2 Cat & GKP cavities — Bosonic cavity qubits — cat and GKP (`cat`)

**The idea.** The qubit is a microwave resonator mode, the Bosonic cavity mode (`cavity`), whose dominant error is photon loss. In the Cat-code encoding (biased noise) two-photon dissipation holds two coherent states apart: bit-flips fall exponentially with mean photon number n̄, phase-flips rise linearly, so a line of d cats, not a d × d patch, corrects the rest; lattice density is the price. The bet: photons buy the distance a lattice buys with qubits, if the bias survives the entangling gate. The alternate GKP grid encoding shares the hardware, not the bet: it corrects small displacements and passes up an analogue syndrome.

**What it needs.** The encoding *requires* two-photon dissipation on a mode, and Bosonic concatenation a biased or GKP inner qubit: the architecture rests on the phase-flip rate during a two-qubit gate. Every measured entangling gate is ancilla-mediated (`g_bos`), through a transmon that does not preserve the bias; the Bias-preserving cat–cat CNOT that would *replace* it is an empty slot with one simulated proposal (Jul 2026) [S][B4]. Six layers offer one station each. Lithographed modes *replace* 3D machined cavities, and the 3D branch *conflicts* with the lattice: centimetre-scale bodies cannot tile a dense 2D array (open [B5]).

**Where it stands.** Seven machines, six devices (Table 8.3): Alice & Bob Boson 4, Helium 2 ("Galvanic Cat"), Helium (on-premise system) and Graphene (planned); AWS Ocelot; Nord Quantique single-mode GKP and Tesseract. The largest, Helium (18 cats), deployed June 2026 without logical data [C][B10]. Bit-flip times reach 430 s (Boson 4) and a preliminary 44-min mean (Helium 2) [C][B1]; only Ocelot ran an outer code: repetition at 1.75% (d = 3, n̄ = 1) and 1.65% (d = 5, n̄ = 1.5) per 2.8 µs cycle, distance and photon number moving together, with CX phase-flip 9.6(4)×10⁻² against bit-flip 3.5(4)×10⁻³ [D][B3]. Helium 2's 5.8×10⁻² is a single-qubit Z gate, not a two-qubit error, so Table 8.3's best two-qubit error on the architecture is Ocelot's 9.6×10⁻² [C][B1]. Nord Quantique single-mode GKP: 8.1×10⁻³ logical error per round, SPAM below 10⁻³ at survival 0.24 × 0.39 [D][B5].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** No off-slot cell, no better-fitting architecture; the shared absences, real-time decoder and interconnect, mark an early stage. The Nord Quantique pair: its decoder cell is an Atlas gap, `∅G-auton` (stabilisation without classical feed-forward), so layer 8 is drawn too narrowly; its code cell reads Bosonic concatenation though neither runs an outer code, like Boson 4, a single cat with zero logical qubits on its roadmap [C][B14]. GKP joins by hardware, not by the bias bet: for its idea the architecture is drawn too widely. Borrowers: Aurora (continuous-variable photonics) takes the GKP and code stations; the dual-rail architecture's Aqumen Seeker, SUSTech biased-erasure cavity qubit and D-Wave gate-model dual-rail system take cavity and gates.

**The next test.** Two expectations to March 2028 (§5.2). First, Helium's roadmap rung, one logical qubit at 10⁻² [R][B14]: phase-flip ≤ 10⁻² per cycle at n̄ ≈ 10 confirms; Helium still without logical data at end-2027 demotes. Second, a measured two-cat gate holding bit-flip time within 10× of idle; if 2028 opens with ancilla-mediated gates only, the 126,133- and 758-cat estimates, which assume 10⁻³ phase-flip per cycle, lose their premise [S][B17][B18]. GKP owes a published GKP–GKP gate.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 7, of them 6 devices, 6 gate-capable).** **Alice & Bob** — Alice & Bob Graphene (planned, 2,000), Alice & Bob Helium (on-premise system) (deployed, 18), Alice & Bob Helium 2 ("Galvanic Cat") (demonstrated, 12), Alice & Bob Boson 4 (demonstrated, 1); **Nord Quantique** — Nord Quantique Tesseract (demonstrated, 2), Nord Quantique single-mode GKP (demonstrated, 1); **AWS Center for Quantum Computing** — AWS Ocelot (demonstrated, 9).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (2).* Nord Quantique single-mode GKP: L8 `∅G-auton` (Atlas gap — no classical decoder: autonomous, feed-forward-free stabilisation with analog closest-vect); Nord Quantique Tesseract: L8 `∅G-auton` (Atlas gap — no classical decoder: autonomous stabilisation with heralded discard; no real-time decoder).

#### 8.3.3 Dual-rail erasure — Dual-rail erasure qubits (`dualrail`)

**The idea.** One excitation shared between two modes, cavity modes or transmons, is the Dual-rail (erasure) encoding (`enc_dualrail`): logical |10⟩ and |01⟩; loss on either rail lands in |00⟩, outside the code space. A Mid-circuit erasure check sees that exit without reading the logical state: the dominant error arrives located, cheaper to correct than an unknown Pauli error. The price: two modes per qubit and a check every cycle. The bet: fail more often, but visibly; with 98% of errors converted, the surface-code threshold rises from 0.94% to 4.15% [S][X8].

**What it needs.** The chain is hard: the encoding *requires* the check, and Erasure-adapted codes *require* its flags; without the check the qubit is merely lossier, without a flag-using decoder the heralds are wasted. The check's tax: 384 ns on transmons, residual 6.0(2)×10⁻⁴ against 2.54(1)×10⁻² erasure per check [D][B8], inside a gate-limited round of at least 2.8 µs [S] (§7.4). Six layers (2–4, 7–9) hold one station each; the gate station *requires* the modes plus a transmon ancilla. Alternates: vertical delivery and on-chip flux-DAC multiplexing *replace* room-temperature lines, lithography *replaces* 3D machining, and transmon rails escape the cavities' *conflict* with a dense lattice.

**Where it stands.** Five machines, three devices (Table 8.3): Aqumen Seeker, eight dual-rail cavity qubits (Quantum Circuits Inc., now D-Wave, 2024) [C][B9][B13]; OQC GENESIS, 16 sites, deployed September 2025; the SUSTech biased-erasure cavity qubit; the AWS dual-rail transmon erasure line, a component; the D-Wave gate-model dual-rail system, 17 qubits due in 2026 [R][B16]. The Yale/Quantum Circuits cavity CZ: ~500 ns, 0.53% erasure per gate, post-selected Pauli error below 0.1%, about 80% of its error flagged [D][B7]. Table 8.3's best two-qubit error, 1.0×10⁻³ on Seeker, excludes heralded erasures; with them it is ~10⁻² [C][B9]. GENESIS, the table's largest gate-capable device, publishes no entangling gate; its gate cell reads undisclosed, not none. No machine has run an erasure-decoded code.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** No off-slot cell and no better-fitting architecture, but two sit loosely. GENESIS's carrier alternate is an Atlas gap, `∅G-dimon`: one fixed-frequency multimode transmon, three islands and two junctions, two transmon-like modes [C][S40], beyond the transmon station (layer 1 too narrow); its gate is undisclosed (a data gap), its code cell none: error detection by post-selection. The SUSTech qubit is not dual-rail: it encodes in the vacuum and two-photon Fock states of one cavity, with erasure bias above 265 [D][S41]; its encoding cell is mislabelled, or the architecture too narrow, its idea being loss-to-erasure, not two rails. The D-Wave system is a hybrid: annealer flux-DAC control. Other architectures borrow the check (Phoenix (Gen-1) and Gen-2, Magne; alkaline-earth Rydberg) and the encoding (Lucy, Belenos; fusion-based photonic), as alternates.

**The next test.** Two expectations to March 2028 (§5.2). First, the D-Wave 17-qubit system publishes a logical error 2× below physical by mid-2027; if end-2027 passes with no dual-rail machine reporting logical error falling more than 2× per distance step on unconditioned data, the architecture is demoted. Second, the first dual-rail memory of at least ten rounds at distance 3, erasure flags in the decoder, survival reported; the CHIPS-backed design at 100 physical qubits per logical [R][B19] presumes it.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 5, of them 3 devices, 2 gate-capable).** **AWS Center for Quantum Computing** — AWS dual-rail transmon erasure line (demonstrated, 2); **D-Wave Quantum** — D-Wave gate-model dual-rail system (announced, 17); **Oxford Quantum Circuits** — OQC GENESIS (deployed, 16); **Quantum Circuits Inc. (acquired by D-Wave, Jan 2026)** — Aqumen Seeker (deployed, 8); **Southern University of Science and Technology** — SUSTech biased-erasure cavity qubit (demonstrated, 1).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (3).* OQC GENESIS: L1 `∅G-dimon` (Atlas gap — Dimon: three islands, two junctions, one dual-rail qubit per device; the transmon node doe); OQC GENESIS: L3 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; SUSTech biased-erasure cavity qubit: L2 `∅G-fock02` (Atlas gap — qubit in \|0⟩/\|2⟩ Fock states of one cavity; photon loss flagged as erasure).

#### 8.3.4 QCCD ions — Trapped ions — QCCD (transport between zones) (`ion_qccd`)

**The idea.** The quantum charge-coupled device (QCCD) keeps every gate in a small, cold crystal and moves the ions instead: electrode voltages carry hyperfine clock qubits through junctions or grid sites (`cx_qccd`) into zones where a Mølmer–Sørensen laser gate acts on two ions [D][I1]. The qubit survives the ride; the motion does not, so every move costs splitting, merging and re-cooling. The bet: trade clock for fidelity and connectivity (any pair meets, at an error no static layout matches), then cut transport time faster than rivals close the fidelity gap. On Helios a two-qubit gate takes ~70 µs, a full-width layer ~55 ms [D][I1].

**What it needs.** Ion shuttling *requires* junction or grid traps, whose electrodes grow with the register: 1,228 on Helios, driven by 273 voltages [D][I1]. High-rate concatenated codes *require* the all-to-all that transport buys; on Helix, moving an ion relabels a qubit, so automorphism gates are free [P][I28]. Carrier, encoding, connectivity, readout and interconnect hold one station each; at gate, control and fabrication the laser and electronic branches are *replaces* pairs, so transport alone defines the architecture. The colour-code alternate *conflicts* with matching. The derived syndrome round: 9.66 ms, 9.04 ms of it transport [S] (§7.4).

**Where it stands.** Seven machines, four counted as devices: Quantinuum's H1-1, H2-1 and Helios and Universal Quantum's iQPU (Helix, a code on Helios, is a component); Sol (2027, 192 qubits) and Apollo are plans [R][I24]. Helios, the largest, holds the best median two-qubit error, 7.9×10⁻⁴ across all pairs, with SPAM 3.3×10⁻⁴ [D][I1]; on it ran 48 error-corrected logical qubits at ~1×10⁻⁴ logical gate error [D][I7] and a Helix memory at 4.6×10⁻⁵ per logical qubit per cycle, not post-selected [P][I28]. The iQPU publishes no gate error; no built machine has an interconnect station.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** Integrated photonic delivery, the architecture's stated direction, is in no built machine: Helios drives each operation with focused free-space beams parallel to the chip surface [D][I1] — so the free-space station is the architecture's primary since 26 September 2026 and integrated photonics its alternate; the H1-1 and H2-1 cells are press-level. Quantinuum's decoders fall outside layer 8: real-time lookup tables on H1-1 and H2-1 (`∅G-lut`), Helix's offline block decoder (`∅G-frontier`). Helix is a code on Helios, not a second trap, and is counted as a component. The iQPU is a hybrid: microwave gate, chip control and foundry fabrication from the electronic-control architecture, transport from this one; it fits both with seven stations each. Its interconnect is the gap `∅G-ionmatter`: ions carried between two chip modules at 2,424 transfers s⁻¹, transport infidelity below 7×10⁻⁸ [D][I36], the architecture's one demonstrated module link, credited in the family's scaling score (§2.3), absent from the map. Transport crosses the module edge; layer 9, photonic only, is drawn too narrowly. The iQPU's fabrication cell reads trap microfabrication on a commercial 200 mm line, the CMOS-foundry station a press-level alternate. Innsbruck and QUARTET borrow ion shuttling as an alternate.

**The next test.** Sol decides it (§5.2): confirm if it ships in 2027 with ≥99.9% two-qubit gates on the 2-D grid and a full-width layer below 10 ms, 5× faster than Helios; demote if it slips past 2027 or its layer stays within 2× of 55 ms: a code demonstrator, not an algorithm runner. Second signal: a third transport-linked module.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 7, of them 4 devices, 4 gate-capable).** **Quantinuum** — Helios (deployed, 98), System Model H2 (H2-1, H2-2) (deployed, 56), Helix architecture on Helios (demonstrated, 20), System Model H1 (H1-1) (deployed, 20), Apollo (planned), Sol (planned); **Universal Quantum** — iQPU / UQConnect (demonstrated, 64).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (4).* System Model H1 (H1-1): L8 `∅G-lut` (Atlas gap — real-time lookup-table decoding of Steane [[7,1,3]] in-loop since 2021; no LUT node among ); System Model H2 (H2-1, H2-2): L8 `∅G-lut` (Atlas gap — real-time lookup-table decoder); Helix architecture on Helios: L8 `∅G-frontier` (Atlas gap — Frontier: block-specific offline decoder for concatenated high-rate codes, neither matchin); iQPU / UQConnect: L9 `∅G-ionmatter` (Atlas gap — chip-to-chip matter link: UQConnect physically transfers ions between trap modules, 2,424 ).

#### 8.3.5 Laser-addressed chain — Trapped ions — linear Paul trap with individual laser addressing (`ion_chain`)

**The idea.** One static chain in a linear Paul trap: its collective motional modes (`cx_bus`) couple every pair, so a Mølmer–Sørensen gate entangles any two ions without moving them, and each ion gets its own acousto-optically steered beam (`ct_ionaod`). The qubit is hyperfine (Yb⁺, Ba⁺) or the optical S–D qubit of ⁴⁰Ca⁺ (`enc_opt`: AQT, Innsbruck, PIAST-Q). It gives up length: N ions share N modes in a fixed bandwidth, so gates slow as the chain grows, to a median 672 µs on Forte's 30 ions [D][I27]. The bet: connectivity is free inside a chain of tens of ions; scale is bought at its edge with photonic links between chains, the modular architecture of Monroe et al. [S][I37].

**What it needs.** The gate *requires* spin–motion coupling and free-space laser fields, which *require* bulk optics. The two defining stations *conflict* (open): mode crowding makes the gate slower and less faithful with each ion; the mitigations (zoned short chains, electronic gates on ≤4-ion segments) belong to other ion architectures. qLDPC and high-rate codes *require* the chain's all-to-all: four logical qubits in 18 ions [D][I11]. Encoding offers three mutually *replacing* stations (hyperfine, optical, metastable); carrier, gate, connectivity, readout and interconnect have one station each; the interconnect, Ion–photon photonic link, carries the scaling bet at a best 250 s⁻¹ [D][I16] against ~10⁴ s⁻¹ needed [S] (§2.3). Layer 10 is drawn too narrowly: planar surface-electrode chips only, while IBEX Q1 runs in a machined blade trap [D][I39] and the 512-ion crystal in a monolithic 3-D trap [D][I17].

**Where it stands.** Thirteen machines, ten devices: IonQ's Aria, Forte and 40-ion Ba-133 qLDPC testbed; AQT's IBEX Q1; PIAST-Q; Sandia's QSCOUT; Qudoor's AbaQ; the Innsbruck and Maryland/Duke traps; Tsinghua's analog 512-ion simulator [D][I17]. Tempo is deployed but not counted: its 100 qubits and "99.9% fidelity" are targets without gate times [C][I27]. Quantum Art and the Rosatom 50-qubit machine are announcements. The largest gate-capable device, the 40-ion testbed, reached memory break-even within error bars, 3.95(68) s logical against 3.84(48) s physical, with leakage post-selection [D][I11]. Maryland/Duke's 3.0×10⁻³ is a logical Clifford error after correction, not a physical figure, and is no longer counted [D][I38]; Table 8.3's best error is therefore the vendor's 4.0×10⁻³ on Aria and Forte [C][I3]. IonQ, the largest vendor, has put its successor, Superion 256, on electronic gates [C][I30].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** Maryland/Duke carries three: that logical figure; a Bacon–Shor subsystem code with no station (`∅G-subsystem`; its high-rate alternate encodes one qubit); a link cell merging a separate experiment, the 250 s⁻¹ record [D][I16], with Oxford's 9.7 s⁻¹ link [D][I15]: a lineage, not a machine. Innsbruck borrows ion shuttling (segmented traps) and the magic-state station [D][I40]: a lineage spanning both laser architectures, and a code layer drawn too narrowly; its measurement-free feedback is a gap (`∅G-coherent`). IBEX Q1's shuttling alternate describes AQT's research traps, not the product: mislabelled. Forte's CMOS-foundry alternate projects IonQ's SkyWater route, first used for the 256-qubit chips of 2026 [C][I30], onto a 2022 machine. The testbed and Tempo decode by offline beam search (`∅G-beam`), outside layer 8; Tempo's code cell repeats the testbed's run. Tsinghua's crystal is analog under global beams (`∅G-isinganalog`, `∅G-crystal`): the architecture is drawn too widely, and ions lack an analog architecture. Quantum Art's link is filed as undisclosed, yet its paper segments one crystal into cells with reconfigurable optical potentials [S][I41]: neither bus nor transport, a gap. Qudoor's control and all Rosatom cells: data gaps. Borrowers: the electronic-control architecture takes the motional bus (JION, QUARTET, Superion 256); planqc's MAQCS the optical qubit.

**The next test.** The link decides: confirm if an ion–photon link exceeds 10³ s⁻¹ by end-2027; demote if none passes ~10× the 250 s⁻¹ record by end-2028. At chain scale, confirm if per-pair errors and gate times appear for Tempo or any chain above 40 ions by end-2027; if disclosure stays at headline level while IonQ ships electronic gates, the architecture narrows to racks and laboratories, leaving early fault tolerance to other ion architectures.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 13, of them 10 devices, 9 gate-capable).** **IonQ** — Tempo (deployed, 100), 40-ion Ba-133 qLDPC testbed (demonstrated, 40), Aria (deployed, 32), Forte / Forte Enterprise (deployed, 30); **Alpine Quantum Technologies** — IBEX Q1 (Marmot / PINE / LYNX) (deployed, 12); **Lebedev Physical Institute (FIAN) and the Russian Quantum Center, Rosatom roadmap** — 50-qubit ion quantum computer (Rosatom roadmap) (announced, 50); **Poznan Supercomputing and Networking Center (PCSS)** — PIAST-Q (deployed, 20); **Quantum Art** — multi-core 2-D ion architecture (announced); **Qudoor (Guokaike)** — AbaQ (deployed); **Sandia National Laboratories** — QSCOUT (Quantum Scientific Computing Open User Testbed) (deployed, 6); **Tsinghua University (Duan group)** — 2-D site-resolved ion simulator (demonstrated, 512); **University of Innsbruck** — University of Innsbruck / IQOQI research trap (demonstrated, 20); **University of Maryland / Duke University** — Maryland / Duke Quantum Center (EURIQA lineage) (demonstrated, 13).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (9).* 40-ion Ba-133 qLDPC testbed: L8 `∅G-beam` (Atlas gap — beam-search decoder (Delfosse) for the qLDPC codes, run offline; no Atlas node for search ); multi-core 2-D ion architecture: L5 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; multi-core 2-D ion architecture: L9 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; AbaQ: L5 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; 2-D site-resolved ion simulator: L3 `∅G-isinganalog` (Atlas gap — global laser-driven spin–spin coupling; analog Ising simulator in a 2-D ion crystal, not a); 2-D site-resolved ion simulator: L4 `∅G-crystal` (Atlas gap — static 2-D Coulomb crystal, power-law all-to-all coupling (511 neighbours), no transport); University of Innsbruck / IQOQI research trap: L4 Ion shuttling (QCCD, junctions, grid traps) (`cx_qccd`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); University of Innsbruck / IQOQI research trap: L8 `∅G-coherent` (Atlas gap — measurement-free: syndromes mapped into an auxiliary register, coherent feedback unitary r); Maryland / Duke Quantum Center (EURIQA lineage): L7 `∅G-subsystem` (Atlas gap — Bacon–Shor [[9,1,3]] subsystem code on 13 ions: FT prep, FT measurement, logical rotation,).

#### 8.3.6 Electronic ion gates — Trapped ions — electronic qubit control (microwave / RF gates) (`ion_elec`)

**The idea.** Take the laser out of the entangling gate: currents in the trap chip's electrodes make a microwave field whose gradient couples a hyperfine ion qubit to the shared motion (`g_elec`); drive traces are shared and local tuning electrodes select the ions [D][I42]. A microwave photon reaches no excited state, so laser gates' scattering floor vanishes: 8.4×10⁻⁵ on two ions, without ground-state cooling [D][I4]. Speed is the price, since a stronger gradient puts the ion nearer heating surfaces: 225.8 µs per gate [D][I4] against ~70 µs on Helios [D][I1]. The bet: control that scales like electronics, ~200 sources for 1,000 ions [S] (§7.6).

**What it needs.** The gate *requires* chip-integrated microwave control (`ct_ionmw`), which *requires* current traces in the trap: the chip is the control system, with no fallback to laser gates. Lasers remain for cooling, state preparation and fluorescence readout. Carrier, gate, control, readout and interconnect have one station each; elsewhere the options *replace* each other: chain bus or shuttling, qLDPC or high-rate codes (both *require* all-to-all), Relay-BP or matching, trap microfabrication or a 300 mm CMOS line. Without transport the derived syndrome round is gate-limited: 1.52 ms, 1.4 ms of it in gates [S] (§7.4).

**Where it stands.** Six machines, two devices, no code, decoder or link run. QUARTET (Oxford Ionics/IonQ), at the UK's National Quantum Computing Centre, is the largest at 32 qubits (Table 8.3), a count no primary source gives; its evidence paper shows a seven-zone chip for up to 10 qubits [D][I42]. JION (eleQtron with Forschungszentrum Jülich), inaugurated on 3 Sep 2026, runs 30 ytterbium ions with no published fidelity [P][I43]. Hence no best error in Table 8.3: the 99.99% is a two-ion record [D][I4]. Superion 256 is orderable: SkyWater-made chips, ions trapped in prototypes, no 256-qubit fidelity [C][I30]; IonQ Superion 10K (commercial 2028) [R][I30] and NFQC-1k (≥1,000 physical, 50 logical qubits by 2030) [R][I34] are plans; QUDORA NFQC is an emulator.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** No cell leaves the architecture's slots; the exceptions are in the evidence. Superion 256 alone fills all ten layers, from plans: a qLDPC code from a resource estimate [S][I29], a claimed photonic link [C][I30], a Relay-BP cell reading "algorithm not named" while IonQ's published decoder runs on one CPU [S][I13] (mislabelled), a metastable-encoding alternate citing nothing. Layers 7–9 are IonQ's intentions. IonQ Superion 10K and NFQC-1k leave four layers undisclosed: early stage. JION's MAGIC (magnetic-gradient-induced coupling) addresses ions by frequency in a static gradient, a second mechanism inside a station defined by the near-field one: drawn too widely. QUARTET's shuttling alternate has no timeline. Universal Quantum's iQPU (QCCD architecture) borrows the gate and control stations and fits both architectures equally: a hybrid.

**The next test.** Superion 256 is due at NVIDIA's research centre and Florida International University in 2027 [C][I31][I32]. Confirm if it is commissioned by end-2027 near 10⁻⁴ two-qubit error over the full register, publishing gate time, heating and crosstalk (§5.2). Demote if it slips beyond 2027, or if its error lands tenfold above the two-ion record once hundreds of microwave channels share a die: electronic control would then remain the single-qubit layer of laser-entangled machines.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 6, of them 2 devices, 2 gate-capable).** **IonQ** — IonQ Superion 10K (planned, 10,000), Superion 256 (announced, 256); **Oxford Ionics (IonQ)** — QUARTET (deployed, 32); **QUDORA Technologies** — QUDORA NFQC (announced); **QUDORA Technologies-led consortium (TU Braunschweig, Leibniz University Hannover, PTB, Forschungszentrum Jülich, NXP Semiconductors Germany, AQT Germany)** — NFQC-1k (planned, 1,000); **eleQtron + Forschungszentrum Julich** — JION (deployed, 30).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (10).* IonQ Superion 10K: L4 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; IonQ Superion 10K: L7 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; IonQ Superion 10K: L8 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; IonQ Superion 10K: L9 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; Superion 256: L8 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; QUDORA NFQC: L4 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; NFQC-1k: L4 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; NFQC-1k: L7 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; NFQC-1k: L8 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; NFQC-1k: L9 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station.

#### 8.3.7 Alkali Rydberg gates — Rydberg tweezer array — alkali (Rb/Cs) (`atom_rb`)

**The idea.** A rubidium or caesium atom in an optical tweezer holds a hyperfine clock qubit, coherent for 12.6 s in a 6,100-atom caesium array [D][N6]. A global laser pulse drives neighbours toward a Rydberg state, where one excitation blocks the other: a CZ gate in ~270 ns [D][N5]. Acousto-optic deflectors (AODs) move atoms or whole code blocks so any pair can meet: connectivity is a program. The price is the clock: transport and imaging, not the gate, set a derived QEC round of 1.31 ms [S] (§7.4), 1–4.5 ms measured [D][N5][N16]. The bet: qubits that cost laser power, not fabrication, and transversal logic between moved blocks, with constant syndrome rounds per logical gate, buying back the transport time [S][N17].

**What it needs.** The gate *requires* the alkali atom and global Rydberg pulses from free-space lasers (`ct_laser`). The high-rate and algorithmic-fault-tolerance codes *require* AOD transport: moving one block onto another pairs every qubit with its partner under one pulse. Algorithmic fault tolerance *requires* correlated, loss-aware decoding, which *requires* transversal circuits; its gain, 1.73(13)× over a loss-blind decoder, rests on four rounds of one run [D][N5], none in real time. Continuous reload *replaces* one-shot loading and *conflicts* with the stored qubits, which see scattered cooling light; a reservoir carried 0.5 m by conveyors mitigates it [D][N10]. Carrier, encoding, gate, interconnect and fabrication hold one station each, code five, decoder four: the hardware is fixed, the code open. No atom–photon cavity link has joined two modules; AOD bandwidth runs out near 10⁴ atoms [S] (§2.4).

**Where it stands.** Thirteen machines, seven devices, five gate-capable. The largest, the 448-atom fault-tolerant processor (Harvard/MIT/QuEra), holds the best two-qubit error, 1.6×10⁻³ [D] (Table 8.3), and ran surface codes d=3→5 with 2.14× suppression over four rounds and up to 96 logical qubits [D][N5]. Gemini-class (QuEra; 99.2% global CZ, one shot per second [C][N4]) ran logical magic-state distillation [D][N12]; Sqale (Infleqtion) entangled 30 logical qubits in error-detecting blocks [C][N28]; Orion Alpha/Beta/Gamma (Pasqal) publishes no gate error. The largest arrays have no gate: the 6,100-qubit tweezer array [D][N6] and the continuously reloaded 3,000-atom machine, run over 2 h [D][N10]. Libra (QuEra, 2028: >256 logical qubits at 10⁻⁶) [R][N24] and Vela (Pasqal) are plans; Google's 2026 track names no species [P][N23]. Five rows have no documented cell: Hanyuan-1, Hanyuan-2, Qinghe-1, the Moscow State University prototype, the Quantum X Labs platform.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** The boundary with the analog simulator architecture (`atom_analog`) runs through layers 2 and 3: carrier, transport, control, readout and fabrication are shared, but the analog qubit is the ground–Rydberg pair and its computation continuous Hamiltonian evolution, with no code, decoder or link above. Pasqal crosses it both ways: Orion, filed here, lists the analog encoding and evolution as alternates; Fresnel, filed there, lists the hyperfine qubit and blockade CZ. One apparatus runs either mode, so the line divides modes, and a machine is filed by its primary one: hybrids, not errors. Orion's chip-optics alternate cites nothing. Gemini-class and Libra list fast readout as an alternate neither machine shows: mislabelled. Libra's ten-layer fit rests on a cavity cell specifying no link. The reload machine is dated August 2026 from a review; its paper is from 2025 [D][N10]. The Moscow prototype has no entangling gate yet a two-qubit error from a press statement; Quantum X Labs names no species; Hanyuan-2's inter-core link is undisclosed: data gaps. The alkaline-earth architecture shares gate, transport, reload, decoder and cavity; carrier and erasure encoding divide them.

**The next test.** Confirm by end-2027 (§5.2): a memory of ≥100 rounds with reloading at Λ>2 (error suppression per code-distance step) on an alkali array, and a gate-capable alkali processor above 1,180 qubits, the family frontier the alkaline-earth architecture has held since 2023 (§8.5, F1b). Demote if Libra slips past 2028, or two-qubit fidelity stalls at 99.9%, the ceiling a 2026 review sets for the standard CZ, with no new gate scheme [S][N7].

**Machines of this architecture (register: 13, of them 7 devices, 5 gate-capable).** **Pasqal** — Vela (announced, 256), Orion Alpha / Beta ("Ruby") / Gamma (deployed, 140); **QuEra** — Libra (planned, 10,000), Gemini-class (deployed, 260); **Zhongke Kuyuan (Wuhan, CAS)** — Hanyuan-2 (announced, 200), Hanyuan-1 (deployed, 100); **Caltech (Endres group)** — 6,100-qubit tweezer array (demonstrated, 6,100); **Harvard / MIT** — continuously reloaded 3,000-atom machine (demonstrated, 3,000); **Harvard / MIT / QuEra** — 448-atom fault-tolerant processor (demonstrated, 448); **Infleqtion** — Sqale (deployed, 100); **Lomonosov Moscow State University with the Russian Quantum Center** — 72-qubit neutral-atom (Rb) quantum computer prototype (50 qubits in Dec 2024) (announced, 72); **Quantum X Labs Inc. (Nasdaq: QXL)** — Quantum X Labs neutral-atom platform (50+ qubits, claimed) (announced, 50); **Zhongqi Wuliang (Shanghai, CAS SIOM spin-off)** — Qinghe-1 (announced).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (4).* Hanyuan-2: L9 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; Orion Alpha / Beta ("Ruby") / Gamma: L2 Ground–Rydberg analog qubit (`enc_gr`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); Orion Alpha / Beta ("Ruby") / Gamma: L3 Analog Rydberg Hamiltonian evolution (`g_rydanalog`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); Libra: L9 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station.

#### 8.3.8 Alkaline-earth arrays — Rydberg tweezer array — alkaline-earth (Yb/Sr), erasure-native (`atom_ae`)

**The idea.** Ytterbium and strontium have a metastable ³P₀ level one clock-line photon above the ground state. Store the qubit there (the Metastable ('omg') erasure encoding on the Alkaline-earth atom (Yb/Sr) — erasure-native), and ground-state imaging light, dark to the qubit, reveals atoms that decayed out of it: errors arrive located, as erasures. The bet: a worse gate for a cheaper code. At 98% conversion the surface-code threshold rises from 0.94% to 4.15% [S][X8]; measured, a metastable CZ errs 0.016(1), 38(6)% flagged, against 0.0060 raw on the ground-state ¹⁷¹Yb qubit [D][N8][N2].

**What it needs.** A relay: the encoding *requires* the metastable manifold; the Mid-circuit erasure check *requires* the encoding, since only a qubit dark to imaging can be checked unread; Erasure-adapted codes *require* the flags and Correlated / loss-aware decoding, since a plain matcher cannot use them. Break a link and the machine is an alkali array with a heavier atom. Five layers have one option; *replaces* edges name the retreats: ground-state or optical encodings, surface code, millisecond imaging, one-shot loading. One *conflict*: reload light (MOT, repumper) depletes stored atoms; a repumper-free MOT and ³P₀ shelving, 13 s lifetime, mitigate it [P][N37].

**Where it stands.** Five machines, three devices (Table 8.3). Phoenix (Gen-1) and Gen-2, 1,180 ¹⁷¹Yb atoms (Oct 2023), is the largest gate-capable device, with the only two-qubit figure, 4.0×10⁻³ [C][N13]; its toric code ran 90 rounds, but suppression vanished with reloading included [D][N13]. Magne, 1,225 physical for ~50 logical, is being installed for early 2027 [R][N22][N29][N30]. The metastable Yb-171 erasure array (Princeton, 20 atoms) slowed [[4,2,2]] decay 1.9(4)× with unconditional erasure decoding [D][N8]. MAQCS and DiNAQC: a 1,000-qubit demonstrator at TRL 5 [C][N33]; the neutral-ytterbium QPU began hardware validation in August 2026 [C][N38]. 28 of 59 cells documented.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** All five fit here best; only Princeton runs the bet's encoding, check, code and decoder as primaries. Phoenix and Magne encode in the ¹⁷¹Yb ground-state nuclear spin (Yaqumo by inference); their erasures are imaged atom losses, as on rubidium [D][N5]. Phoenix's metastable-encoding cell is denied by its own note (mislabelled), its code is an Atlas gap, `∅G-toric` (code station too narrow), its row joins ⁸⁷Sr and ¹⁷¹Yb generations. MAQCS encodes on the Sr clock line, 10–100 ms coherence [P][N26], in an optical lattice (`∅G-lattice`, shared with QUIONE), no code, an undisclosed core link; planqc's error-corrected line, LOGIQC, is ytterbium [C][N34]. Yaqumo's fast readout is inferred from 17.6 µs imaging of spinless ¹⁷⁴Yb [P][N39]: early stage. No machine has the Atom–photon cavity interface. Borrowers: OQC GENESIS (check), Superion 256 (encoding), IBEX Q1 (optical qubit), QUIONE (carrier). The architecture is drawn by species; erasure-native rests on one laboratory.

**The next test.** Consistent with §5.2: by mid-2027 Magne crosses the 1,180-qubit frontier (§8.5, F1b) and delivers 50 logical qubits, decoded with erasure flags on unconditioned data; loss detection alone confirms the carrier, not the encoding. By end-2027 a group outside Princeton and Caltech converts over 70% of a metastable CZ's error with residual below 3×10⁻³; if none passes 50% and the next Yb memory again loses suppression under reloading, the architecture demotes to a species choice within the alkali bet.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 5, of them 3 devices, 3 gate-capable).** **Atom Computing** — Phoenix (Gen-1) and Gen-2 (deployed, 1,180); **Atom Computing + Microsoft, for QuNorth** — Magne (announced, 1,225); **Princeton (Thompson group)** — metastable Yb-171 erasure array (demonstrated, 20); **Yaqumo (+ Fujitsu)** — neutral-ytterbium QPU (demonstrated); **planqc** — MAQCS and DiNAQC (planned, 1,000).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (3).* Phoenix (Gen-1) and Gen-2: L7 `∅G-toric` (Atlas gap — toric code d=4 and d=6, up to 90 syndrome-extraction rounds; lower logical error at larger); MAQCS and DiNAQC: L4 `∅G-lattice` (Atlas gap — no Atlas node: optical-lattice grid ('egg carton made of light'), no per-atom tweezer move); MAQCS and DiNAQC: L9 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station.

#### 8.3.9 Analog atom simulator — Neutral-atom analog simulator (Rydberg arrays, lattice gases) (`atom_analog`)

**The idea.** Keep the alkali tweezer array with its lasers, transport and imaging; drop the gate: global Rydberg lasers drive the whole register under one Ising Hamiltonian, Analog Rydberg Hamiltonian evolution (`g_rydanalog`) on the Ground–Rydberg analog qubit (`enc_gr`). Waveforms Ω(t), Δ(t) and atom geometry are the program, a bit-string snapshot the output. The bet: for many-body physics (G1), native Hamiltonian evolution reaches hundreds of atoms now. It gives up memory, gates and any route to fault tolerance.

**What it needs.** The evolution *requires* the ground–Rydberg basis, a Rydberg level in the carrier and global Rydberg lasers; imaging, an imaging transition. Code, decoder and interconnect are empty by design (§7.7): here analog simulation stops being a quantum computer in the register's sense. The encoding is a basis, not a memory: |r⟩ cannot be shelved; Aquila's T2* is 5.8 µs, programs stop at 4 µs [C][N40]; errors integrate coherently with no instant for a syndrome, so no code attaches and no decoder has input. Without an entangling gate they are devices, not processors (Table 8.3 prints no gate-capable size, no two-qubit error). The one *replaces* edge swaps the species.

**Where it stands.** Three machines, three devices, none gate-capable. Aquila (QuEra, 256 Rb-87 atoms, on Amazon Braket since November 2022) [C][N40] carries the physics: (2+1)D string breaking in a gauge theory, no advantage claim [D][A6]. Fresnel / Fresnel 2 (Pasqal, deployed 2023–2024) publishes no error rates. QUIONE (ICFO, April 2024) images an ⁸⁴Sr quantum gas site by site in an 813.4 nm lattice, fidelity above 94% [D][N41]. §7.4's T₂ for the architecture is the ground–Rydberg qubit's own 11.4 µs (Aquila, spin echo) [C][N40] since 26 Sep 2026, no longer the carrier's 12.6-s caesium hyperfine record [N6], which a qubit with a Rydberg level cannot use. 25 of 33 cells documented. No machine postdates 2024; the vendors' later ones (Gemini-class, Libra, Orion, Vela) are gate-model.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** The dual-mode Pasqal machines mirror each other: Fresnel / Fresnel 2 runs analog as primary, its digital mode (Hyperfine / clock-state qubit, Rydberg-blockade CZ) and PIC-generated tweezers as alternates; Orion Alpha / Beta ("Ruby") / Gamma, on the alkali architecture, swaps the modes; all else matches. Each fits its own architecture seven stations to five: the boundary with atom_rb is a mode switch in layers 2–3 of one hardware (hybrid). The gate separates simulator from processor; the line fault tolerance needs, a code, neither has crossed. QUIONE lacks encoding, gate and Rydberg state: a Bose–Hubbard gas in a lattice (`∅G-lattice`, shared with MAQCS) under a carrier named for erasure-native tweezer qubits, here by elimination (four stations in slot): the architecture is too narrow for lattice quantum-gas simulators. Caltech's erasure-excising Sr Rydberg simulator [N9], which the new alkaline-earth edges describe, is not in the register.

**The next test.** Two expectations; §5.2 has no analog row. Confirmation: by 31 December 2027 a commercial analog machine publishes a many-body fidelity at 60 or more atoms or a T2* above 20 µs, or an analog result survives a year of classical challenge (§2.8). Demotion: if Vela and QuEra's next systems ship without analog specifications and no analog-first machine is announced by mid-2027, analog simulation is a mode, analog alternates of layers 2–3 on atom_rb, not an architecture.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 3, of them 3 devices, 0 gate-capable).** **ICFO - Institut de Ciencies Fotoniques** — QUIONE (demonstrated); **Pasqal** — Fresnel / Fresnel 2 (deployed, 100); **QuEra** — Aquila (deployed, 256).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (4).* QUIONE: L4 `∅G-lattice` (Atlas gap — atoms held in a 2-D in-plane optical lattice plus light sheet at 813.4 nm; no per-atom mov); Fresnel / Fresnel 2: L2 Hyperfine / clock-state qubit (`enc_hf`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); Fresnel / Fresnel 2: L3 Rydberg-blockade CZ (`g_ryd`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); Fresnel / Fresnel 2: L5 PIC-generated tweezers / integrated optics for atoms (`ct_pic_trap`, alternate borrowed from another architecture).

#### 8.3.10 Photon fusion — Fusion-based photonic (FBQC) (`ph_fusion`)

**The idea.** Single photons in path or time-bin encoding are never joined by a gate: Linear-optical fusion (`g_fusion`) measures two photons from small pre-made resource states in the Bell basis, destroying both, and Fusion-based fault tolerance reads the logical state from the pattern of outcomes. The fact exploited: a photon does not decohere, loss is its only error, and a failed fusion announces itself: an erasure at a known place. The price is determinism. The bet: a foundry can build fault tolerance if each photon loses under 2.7% end to end, the threshold with 6-ring resource states [S][P10].

**What it needs.** Fusion *requires* number-resolving detection to herald and switching with feed-forward to route the next photon within its flight time; the switches *require* the Photonic IC foundry, where each costs 0.1–0.2 dB of a ~0.5 dB per-photon budget [D][P1]. The code *requires* the Multi-photon resource-state factory (`src_resource`), an empty slot: fused states reach 8 photons at under 1 Hz against 24–168 photons at MHz [D] (§7.7). A 2026 re-analysis adds a logical-error floor set by fusion failure alone [S][P23]. Seven layers hold one station each, and nothing inside the architecture *replaces* or *conflicts*; fusion itself forecloses the continuous-variable branch, *replacing* Gaussian gates as counting *replaces* homodyne detection.

**Where it stands.** Five machines, three devices (Table 8.3), no logical qubit. Omega chipset (PsiQuantum, February 2025), all ten stations in slot, is a component set and is counted as such, not as a device: fusion Bell fidelity 99.22%, chip-to-chip Bell 99.72% over 42 m [D][P1]; its 7.8×10⁻³ is that single fusion, and its code and decoder cells are flagged no-QEC-cycle. Belenos and Lucy (Quandela, 12 qubits each; Lucy at CEA, 2025 [P7]) are the largest gate-capable devices; their 6.2×10⁻² is Ascella's 2023 figure [D][P24], and Lucy has 0 of 14 cells documented. The ETRI 8-photon silicon-photonic qubit chip publishes no error rate; Carina (QuiX Quantum, 8 qubits) awaits commissioning at DLR [P8].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** No cell falls off the architecture, yet it is drawn wider than its bet. Belenos, Lucy and the ETRI chip occupy the fusion station with post-selected or probabilistic gates in a reconfigurable mesh, circuit-model linear optics that the register's own cells call "not FBQC fusion"; only Omega and Carina fuse. Quandela's fault-tolerance design is a hybrid of quantum emitters and linear-optical gates [P25]. Both Quandela machines carry an Atlas gap, `∅G-twofab`: a III-V quantum-dot source and a SiN/silica mesh, two fabrication stations where the map allows one (layer 10 too narrow). Omega and Belenos list the resource-state factory, an empty slot, as an alternate station; Belenos's own cell calls the route undemonstrated (mislabelled). The other four have no code, decoder or interconnect: early stage. On other architectures, Aurora, Jiuzhang 4.0 and Borealis use its detector station.

**The next test.** By March 2028 (§5.2), a chain of three or more fusions, or a resource state beyond Bell pairs fused into a network, published with an end-to-end success rate, would show that the architecture's real clock, resource-state supply, exists. Demote if only single-fusion figures remain, fused states stay at eight photons, PsiQuantum's Stage C validation [P12] comes back negative, or its 2027 target moves.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 5, of them 3 devices, 3 gate-capable).** **Quandela** — Belenos (deployed, 12), Lucy (deployed, 12); **ETRI (with KAIST and the University of Trento)** — ETRI 8-photon silicon-photonic qubit chip (demonstrated, 8); **PsiQuantum** — Omega chipset (Moreton Bay / Chicago systems unnamed) (demonstrated); **QuiX Quantum** — Carina (announced, 8).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (2).* Belenos: L10 `∅G-twofab` (Atlas gap — needs two L10 nodes: SiN/silica interferometer meshes (fab_pic) plus GaAs quantum-dot sour); Lucy: L10 `∅G-twofab` (Atlas gap — needs two L10 nodes: SiN/silica interferometer meshes (fab_pic) plus GaAs quantum-dot sour).

#### 8.3.11 Optical GKP — Continuous-variable photonic — GKP (`ph_cv`)

**The idea.** Squeezed light modes (`squeezed`), not single photons, carry the computation: beamsplitters entangle them deterministically into a time-multiplexed cluster state, CV Gaussian gates (`g_cv`) compute on it by homodyne measurement and feed-forward, and GKP grid encoding (`enc_gkp`) supplies both the qubit and the non-Gaussian resource that Gaussian operations lack. Xanadu's blueprint builds "three-dimensional hybrid resource states comprising both bosonic qubits and squeezed vacuum states" [S][P22]. Against fusion it trades probabilistic entangling for deterministic, and heralded erasures for analogue noise: loss acts as a random displacement that spends squeezing. The bet: photonic chips will make GKP states near the ~10 dB of squeezing the code needs before loss consumes them [S][P5].

**What it needs.** Gaussian gates *require* squeezed modes and room-temperature feed-forward; homodyne readout *requires* a local oscillator phase-locked by the same electronics, so detection, decision and modulation must close inside the 1 µs clock [D][P4]. Optical GKP *requires* that squeezing; the best on-chip GKP source reaches 0.62 dB effective [D][P5]. The code, bosonic concatenation with GKP under an outer qLDPC code, *requires* the GKP inner qubit, so it waits on that gap. Seven layers hold one station each. The *replaces* edges fix the architecture's identity: Gaussian gates against fusion, homodyne against photon counting, Relay-BP against matching.

**Where it stands.** One machine, one device: Aurora (Xanadu, January 2025), 35 chips, 12 modes per 1 MHz cycle, a 12×N cluster state generated for two hours at ~14 dB total loss, no error-corrected computation [D][P4]. It runs the only in-loop decoder in photonics, within the 1 µs clock [D][P4], and 11 of its 12 cells are documented, the best-evidenced photonic machine. Table 8.3 still credits the architecture with no gate-capable device and no two-qubit error; the register flags Aurora detection-only and loss-dominated. Xanadu puts its loss 24× above threshold in 2026 [C][P9] and plans 1× in 2030, a fault-tolerance milestone in 2028–29 and 1,000+ logical qubits by 2031 [R][P9]; it holds a DARPA QBI Stage B slot [S28].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** An architecture of one machine cannot show whether it is drawn well; it is drawn around one design. Aurora fits every slot, with no gap and no none, but its alternates amend the edges. Number-resolving detection heralds its GKP states, so the architecture needs both detector stations its *replaces* edge treats as rivals: counting to make the qubit, homodyne to compute. Its decoder is matching, with Relay-BP, the architecture's primary for the qLDPC outer code, as alternate. The gate alternate, fusion, has no machine. Jiuzhang 4.0 and Borealis, Aurora's predecessor, share the squeezed carrier and hold the Gaussian-gate station as an off-architecture alternate without feed-forward: a Gaussian sampler is this architecture minus feed-forward and GKP states. Encoding and code are shared with the microwave cat-and-GKP architecture.

**The next test.** By March 2028 (§5.2): an on-chip optical GKP state above 1 dB effective squeezing, and a published loss factor within 10× of threshold; Xanadu's own schedule, 24× in 2026 to 1× in 2030, needs a gain of ~2.2× a year [S]. Both would keep the 2028–29 milestone credible. Demote if effective squeezing stays below 1 dB through 2027, or the milestone moves.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 1, of them 1 devices, 0 gate-capable).** **Xanadu** — Aurora (demonstrated, 12).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots:* none — every cell of every machine sits in a slot of the architecture.

#### 8.3.12 Boson sampling — Boson sampler (`ph_sampler`)

**The idea.** Squeezed light or single photons enter a Programmable linear-optical interferometer (`g_lointer`) and are counted at its outputs; the output probabilities are hafnians of matrices built from the network (permanents for single photons), #P-hard to compute [G][P28]. No entangling gate, no feed-forward, no qubit: the only claim is that no classical computer reproduces the samples (goal G1). Where the fusion architecture builds qubits from small resource states and the continuous-variable architecture encodes them in GKP grids, this architecture skips the qubit. The bet: at high photon number the statistics outrun classical samplers before loss hands the output back to them.

**What it needs.** The interferometer *requires* light in the mesh and room-temperature electro-optic phase shifters; Bulk-optics and fibre assembly (`fab_bulk`) *requires* fibre-coupled modulators and delay loops. The carriers *replace* each other, as do photon counting and homodyne detection, and bulk assembly and the photonic IC foundry. Everything else is transmission: noisy Gaussian boson sampling becomes efficiently simulable once squeezing, transmission and detector efficiency fall below a bound [S][P29]. Code, decoder and interconnect are ∅ by design (§7.3): a sample has no logical state to protect, so loss is modelled rather than corrected, and classical challengers exploit it [P6].

**Where it stands.** Three machines, three devices, none gate-capable (Table 8.3). Jiuzhang 4.0 (USTC, May 2026): 1,024 squeezed states in 8,176 modes, up to 3,050 detected photons, 51% system efficiency, one sample per 25.6 µs [D][P6]. Borealis (Xanadu, 2022): 216 squeezed modes through three fibre loops, TES number-resolving detectors, up to 219 photons, one sample per 36 µs [D][P27]; the register flags its claim eroded. PT-2 (ORCA, deployed 2024–26) samples photon-subtracted squeezed states in a two-loop time-bin interferometer with a number-resolving detector [P][P26]; ORCA publishes no mode count, clock or loss. For the register the ∅ layers mean three things: no sampler enters the gate, code or decoder counts; their nine none cells in layers 7–9 are the architecture, not a delay, and inflate Table 8.2's maturity profile; three of photonics' eight devices (Table 8.1) are samplers, so the family's device count overstates its processors.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** Jiuzhang 4.0 and Borealis hold CV Gaussian gates, the continuous-variable architecture's station, as an off-slot layer-3 alternate: a Gaussian interferometer without homodyne feed-forward. That is lineage, not mislabelling; Borealis's successor is Aurora. Both leave layer 2 none. The encoding station, Time-bin / path photonic encoding, rests on PT-2 alone and fits badly: PT-2 fills every time bin, nominally one photon each [P][P26], so its bins are modes, not the two-bin qubit the station defines; and the station *requires* a single-photon carrier, while PT-2's is squeezed light. No machine uses the single-photon or homodyne alternates either: in layers 1, 2 and 6 the architecture is drawn wider than its register.

**The next test.** By 31 December 2027: no published classical sampler matches Jiuzhang 4.0 on its own benchmarks, and ORCA publishes PT-3's modes, loops and loss with a classical comparison. Demote if a classical algorithm matches Jiuzhang 4.0, as one matched each earlier advantage claim, or if PT-3, due in 2026 and undelivered (§2.5), ships without those figures.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 3, of them 3 devices, 0 gate-capable).** **ORCA Computing** — PT-2 (deployed); **USTC (Pan Jianwei group)** — Jiuzhang 4.0 (demonstrated); **Xanadu** — Borealis (deployed).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (2).* Jiuzhang 4.0: L3 CV Gaussian gates + GKP-assisted non-Gaussian ops (`g_cv`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); Borealis: L3 CV Gaussian gates + GKP-assisted non-Gaussian ops (`g_cv`, alternate borrowed from another architecture).

#### 8.3.13 CMOS spin qubits — Silicon / germanium quantum-dot spins (`spin_qd`)

**The idea.** A Gate-defined quantum-dot spin (`qd_spin`) is a transistor-sized gate stack: printed by a 300 mm CMOS foundry (`fab_cmos`), entangled by a pulse on the barrier to its neighbour, the Exchange gate (`g_exch`), read by Spin-to-charge conversion (`ro_s2c`), and coherent 1.31 ms (Hahn echo) on foundry ²⁸Si [D][Q7]. The bet: dots uniform enough to share calibration, with control moved into the cryostat, scale like transistors. The price: a readout-limited clock, 8.5 µs derived [S] against ~100–300 µs as run [G] (§7.4), and reach only to neighbours unless spins move.

**What it needs.** Exchange *requires* the dots and baseband pulses: its strength depends exponentially on barrier voltage, and HRL puts ~80% of its CNOT error on control and calibration [D][Q2]. Carrier, cryo-CMOS, conveyor and the Cryogenic signal fan-out all *require* the foundry; the fan-out also *requires* cryo-CMOS behind it: Intel's Pando Tree serves up to 64 qubit terminals at 10–20 mK from one signal and nine digital lines of Horse Ridge II, 0.156 lines per terminal [C][Q30]. The surface code *requires* a 2-D nearest-neighbour lattice, which no register device shows; qLDPC *conflicts* with it (long-range checks), needing the conveyor that *replaces* it. Crossbar lines *conflict* with per-pair calibration. Six layers hold one station; none links modules.

**Where it stands.** Nine machines, five devices (Table 8.3): demonstrated, the HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots — the largest gate-capable device, added to the register on 26 Sep 2026 [D][Q2]), Tunnel Falls (Intel; 12 qubits [D][Q9]) and the foundry 8-qubit array (Diraq/imec, exchange in one of four pairs [D][Q7]); deployed, Bell-1 (UnityQ) (Equal1) and QM-One (Quantum Motion, count unpublished [Q10]); announced, the Diraq 8-qubit silicon spin quantum computer at Equinix Sydney, Groove Quantum and the Hitachi x Intel 18A spin programme; planned, Alloy Pioneer (Quobly), cloud access by end-2026 [R][Q16]. The best two-qubit error among devices, 3.0×10⁻³, is HRL's [D][Q2]; Equal1's product page claims 99.3% (7.0×10⁻³) [C][Q31] where a press survey prints 98.4% [P][Q32]; no other machine publishes one. Off-register bests: 99.04–99.56% on foundry two-qubit devices [D][Q1]. HRL's bit-flip repetition code, error falling 4.7× from distance 3 to 5, sequenced from 4 K, is the architecture's only error-correction run [D][Q2].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** No off-slot cell or better-fitting architecture. Until 26 Sep 2026 the primaries described an absent machine — all eight rows used Single-spin (Loss–DiVincenzo) encoding, none Exchange-only encoding; HRL's device, now IBM's [Q18], is now the ninth row and the architecture's only carrier of its primary encoding, with its repetition code and parity decoder as Atlas gaps (`∅G-repetition`, `∅G-parity`). The foundry 8-qubit array holds the only code cell, a roadmap qLDPC with shuttling as alternate (early stage), and carries 1.1×10⁻² from other devices at 1 K [D][Q5]. Bell-1's on-die control is an Atlas gap (`∅G-qsoc`), QM-One's is not. Tunnel Falls mixes cryo-CMOS and room-temperature drive (hybrid); Equinix control is undisclosed. Only Tunnel Falls documents its fan-out; three room-temperature machines should read none. Five drive single spins by ESR or EDSR, a station only the colour-centre architecture holds: layer 3 is too narrow. Borrowers: cryo-CMOS on five superconducting machines; the foundry on thirteen, eight ion traps.

**The next test.** By March 2028 (§5.2): a 300 mm array above 20 qubits with all-pairs two-qubit fidelity ≥ 99.5% and µs-class readout confirms; Diraq's 2029 target (150 k physical, 1 k logical [R][Q14]) cut again demotes. If the Equinix system (due October 2026 [C][Q20]) and Alloy Pioneer reach 2028 without their own two-qubit error, the best number stays a vendor's.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 9, of them 5 devices, 5 gate-capable).** **Diraq** — foundry 8-qubit array (demonstrated, 8); **Diraq (with Equinix)** — Diraq 8-qubit silicon spin quantum computer at Equinix Sydney (announced, 8); **Equal1** — Bell-1 (UnityQ) (deployed, 6); **Groove Quantum** — Groove Quantum (no named machine) (announced, 18); **HRL Laboratories (IBM since 26 Aug 2026)** — HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots) (demonstrated, 18); **Hitachi + Intel** — Hitachi x Intel 18A spin programme (announced, 100); **Intel** — Tunnel Falls (demonstrated, 12); **Quantum Motion** — QM-One (deployed); **Quobly** — Alloy Pioneer (Quobly) (planned, 100).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (6).* Diraq 8-qubit silicon spin quantum computer at Equinix Sydney: L5 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; Bell-1 (UnityQ): L5 `∅G-qsoc` (Atlas gap — Quantum System on Chip: qubits and control/readout electronics on the same 22FDX die at 0.); HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots): L7 Error-detection codes ([[4,2,2]], d = 2 surface, spacetime) (`code_detect`, primary off the architecture's slots); HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots): L7 `∅G-repetition` (Atlas gap — d=5 bit-flip repetition code on 7 exchange-only qubits, Λ5/3 = 4.7 over up to 200 rounds, ); HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots): L8 `∅G-parity` (Atlas gap — repetition-code syndromes interpreted after the run with a parity-check (majority) decoder); QM-One: L5 `∅G-qsoc` (Atlas gap — cryo-CMOS integrated on the qubit die).

#### 8.3.14 Donor spins — Donor spins in silicon (`spin_donor`)

**The idea.** A Donor spin (P in ²⁸Si) (`donor`), written atom by atom by STM hydrogen lithography (`fab_stm`): the ³¹P nucleus is the data qubit, the bound electron its drive, coupler and readout probe. The fact exploited: a nucleus has no orbital degree of freedom, hence no first-order coupling to the charge noise that holds foundry dots at 99.04–99.56% [D][Q1]; every gate still passes through the electron, which has one. The bet (Table 8.3): the longest coherence in a solid is worth a write process with no foundry.

**What it needs.** Ten layers, one station each, no alternates. The donor *requires* STM placement because exchange between donor electrons falls exponentially with separation, so a donor a few nanometres off leaves a dead register [G]. Exchange *requires* baseband gate pulses; readout *requires* a charge sensor beside the electron; the surface code *requires* a 2-D lattice, which two registers of four and five nuclei [D][Q3] do not form. Two *replaces* edges, dot vs donor and foundry vs STM, separate it from the quantum-dot architecture; the other eight stations are shared. The borrowed fan-out *requires* a CMOS router die and cryo-CMOS control, both absent here: a large array would be a hybrid with the dot architecture.

**Where it stands.** One machine, one device: the 11-qubit donor processor (Silicon Quantum Computing), demonstrated: nine ³¹P nuclear qubits in two registers with two electron ancillas; an electron CROT at 99.64% in 1.25 µs, 3.6×10⁻³, the family's best among devices (§8.4, H2); single-qubit gates to 99.99%; GHZ states over eight nuclei [D][Q3]. Nuclear Hahn echo reaches 660 ms [P][Q27]. No code runs; readout is non-demolition through the electron; control sits at room temperature. Six of ten cells documented. SQC holds a QBI Stage B slot [S28]; its roadmap dates no intermediate milestone. Flag: no foundry route.

**Exceptions and borrowings.** A single machine cannot test the architecture's drawing; SQC, fitting no other architecture better, amends layers 3 to 5. Inside a register the nuclei couple all-to-all through the shared electron, a hub rather than a lattice; nearest neighbour holds only between registers (layer 4 too narrow). The nuclear gates are hyperfine-conditional rotations driven by ESR and NMR, not exchange, which serves only the electron CROT; baseband control excludes that resonant drive: the colour-centre architecture's Microwave / optical spin gates (`g_mwspin`) fit better (layers 3 and 5 too narrow). The fan-out cell records the missing slot, not a component (data gap: none). The manufacturing cell's SkyWater partnership is no foundry route: SkyWater's stated role is superconducting resonators and packaging [C][Q28] and it has been IonQ's since 31 July 2026 [C][I21]. No other architecture uses donor or STM; single-spin encoding and baseband control recur on colour-centre machines and on Majorana 1 (and Majorana 2).

**The next test.** By March 2028 (§5.2 names no donor milestone): a donor device beyond eleven qubits, or a placement-yield distribution for SQC's automated patterning, hours to minutes per 70-dot device and hundreds of thousands of dots patterned [C][Q29], confirms the write step scales; eleven qubits and no yield figure then demote it to a physics testbed. QBI Stage C would be the first independent check.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 1, of them 1 devices, 1 gate-capable).** **Silicon Quantum Computing** — 11-qubit donor processor (demonstrated, 11).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots:* none — every cell of every machine sits in a slot of the architecture.

#### 8.3.15 Spin–photon nodes — Colour-centre spins — network nodes (NV/SiV/T) (`defect`)

**The idea.** A point defect in diamond (NV, SiV, SnV) or silicon (T centre) (`defect`) has a spin-selective optical transition: its electron spin emits a photon entangled with it, and nearby nuclear spins under hyperfine-conditional microwave gates (`g_mwspin`) keep a small memory. Heralded photons (`ic_spinphoton`), not on-chip wiring, join these few-qubit nodes. Table 8.3's bet fuses two on one carrier: a room-temperature NV accelerator and cryogenic network nodes. Both forgo a dense lattice; for the nodes, link rate, not gate error, is the clock.

**What it needs.** The own stations *require* the carrier — gates its spin register, fluorescence readout its optical transition, the link its spin–photon interface — and the carrier *requires* a host crystal, whose only station is diamond (`fab_diamond`). Five stations are borrowed: encoding, readout, baseband control, nearest-neighbour lattice, qLDPC code. Two clash: the code *conflicts* with the lattice, needing degree-6 checks that here only the optical link supplies. No decoder (§7.7): a code named, none decoded.

**Where it stands.** Three machines, all devices, no two-qubit error (Table 8.3). The largest, Quoll / QB-QDK 2.0 (Quantum Brilliance), is deployed: 6 qubits in the register, room-temperature units at Oak Ridge, no gate fidelity [C][Q24]. Entanglement First modules (Photonic Inc, QBI Stage B [S28]) teleported a state into a remote matter node over 30 km of commercial fibre (February 2026), no rate or fidelity [P][Q25]. Fujitsu's Diamond-spin quantum computer prototype carries SnV centres on photonic circuits at −271.6 °C, no qubit count or fidelity, multi-module by 2027 [C][R][Q21]. Per-node figures: NV gate errors below 0.1% [P], SiV entanglement over 35 km at ~1 Hz [D], a post-selected T-centre teleported CNOT [P] [Q13].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** All three fit here best; none fits whole. Photonic connects any-to-any through the optical link (`∅G-optlink`), which its SHYPS qLDPC code needs, claimed at 20× fewer physical qubits per logical [C][Q26]; its silicon host has no station (`∅G-sicolour`): layers 4 and 10 are drawn too narrowly. Quoll lacks link, code and decoder, and its microwave-and-laser control has no station (`∅G-nvroom`): a processor on a network architecture, drawn too widely. Fujitsu's prototype adds a photonic-IC foundry (`fab_pic`), states no connectivity and shares the control gap at 1.55 K: an early hybrid. Only Photonic uses the namesake link; no other architecture borrows the architecture's own stations.

**The next test.** By the station briefs (§5.2 has no defect row), confirmation by March 2028 is an unconditional inter-node gate above 90%, a heralded link above 1 kHz, or link rate and fidelity from Fujitsu's multi-module prototype. Demotion: Photonic's links still without rate and fidelity, and the SnV modules without a qubit count, at end-2027; then the network bet is repeater and sensing technology; Quoll needs its own architecture.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 3, of them 3 devices, 3 gate-capable).** **Fujitsu (with TU Delft and QuTech)** — Diamond-spin quantum computer prototype (demonstrated); **Photonic Inc.** — Entanglement First modules (demonstrated); **Quantum Brilliance** — Quoll / QB-QDK 2.0 (deployed, 6).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (6).* Diamond-spin quantum computer prototype: L4 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; Diamond-spin quantum computer prototype: L5 `∅G-nvroom` (Atlas gap — light, microwave and RF control of SnV spins; module operated at -271.6 °C (1.55 K)); Diamond-spin quantum computer prototype: L10 Photonic IC foundry (SiN, BTO, TFLN, SNSPD on 300 mm) (`fab_pic`, alternate borrowed from another architecture); Entanglement First modules: L4 `∅G-optlink` (Atlas gap — any-to-any optical link used as the connectivity layer, not only as interconnect; basis fo); Entanglement First modules: L10 `∅G-sicolour` (Atlas gap — Si-28 T-centre + silicon-photonics co-fabrication; no foundry node published; fab_diamond ); Quoll / QB-QDK 2.0: L5 `∅G-nvroom` (Atlas gap — room-temperature microwave drive + green-laser initialisation + optical readout; no cryoge).

#### 8.3.16 Majorana parity — Topological — tetron (Majorana) (`topo`)

**The idea.** Four Majorana zero modes on two gate-defined InAs–superconductor wires (`majorana`) hold one qubit at fixed total fermion parity (`enc_parity`, the tetron): Z and X are parities of different mode pairs. Gates would be parity-measurement sequences read by rf quantum capacitance (`g_mbq`, `ro_qcap`). The claim is a topological gap: with the modes far apart, their splitting falls exponentially with wire length and only an arriving quasiparticle flips a parity. Table 8.3's bet, protection in hardware instead of a code, is an existence claim, at the price of Clifford-only gates and no fallback.

**What it needs.** One chain, no alternates: the gate *requires* the wires and the readout, every gate being a measurement; the encoding two wires per tetron; the readout the island's quantum capacitance; the wires an InAs–Pb stack from III-V MBE (`fab_mbe`), whose disorder can mimic Majorana signatures. No *replaces* or *conflicts* edge: with no rival at any layer, one failure strands the architecture. Code, decoder and interconnect are empty (§7.7); a Floquet or measurement-based code awaits a demonstrated qubit. The gate is an empty slot: specified since 2008, never operated.

**Where it stands.** One machine, no device: Majorana 1 (and Majorana 2) (Microsoft), contested, 5/30 in §2.7 [D]. Measured: single-wire parity readout at 1% assignment error, not by itself proof of Majorana modes, the editors noted [D][T1][T2]; tetron Z- and X-loop parity lifetimes of 12.4 ms and 14.5 µs, the X–Z succession test deferred [P][T3]; a ~20 s InAs–Pb parity switching time in one wire of one tetron, called a qubit lifetime by Microsoft's blog [P][T4][C][T5]. No braiding, X measurement on Majorana 2, or two-qubit operation. A Nature Matters Arising disputes the topological reading, answered without concession [D][T8]; QuTech's independent Kitaev-chain parity qubit has limited protection [P][T7].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** None off-slot: the machine fills all seven stations and fits no other architecture — weak evidence: the architecture was drawn around it; one machine cannot test its width. The code gap (`∅G-pathcode`) is telling: Microsoft's own geometric codes need all-to-all connectivity, which its neutral-atom product offers (§3.3) and the intended tetron lattice does not. Of its own stations only MBE travels: Belenos and Lucy (Quandela), on the fusion architecture, grow quantum-dot photon sources with it.

**The next test.** Per §5.2, an X-parity lifetime near the Z lifetime, or any two-qubit entanglement, confirms; neither by end-2027 demotes. Since 22 September 2026 DARPA's evaluators have on-site access to a Majorana 2 system in Microsoft's Maryland research centre — access, not a result [C][T10]. Expectation: by March 2028 an X/Z lifetime ratio below 10² (now ~10³ [T3]) or a joint parity measurement across two tetrons, confirmed by DARPA; without either, the tetron is a materials result, not an architecture.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 1, of them 0 devices, 0 gate-capable).** **Microsoft** — Majorana 1 (and Majorana 2) (other).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (1).* Majorana 1 (and Majorana 2): L7 `∅G-pathcode` (Atlas gap — empty L7 on topo architecture; MS 4D geometric codes [[96,6,8]] need all-to-all, not a tet).

#### 8.3.17 Flux-qubit annealer — Quantum annealer — flux qubits (`anneal`)

**The idea.** Thousands of rf-SQUID flux qubits (`fluxq`) serve as programmable Ising spins: a loop's persistent current, one way or the other, is the spin; inductive couplers set the interactions; the chip evolves continuously (`g_anneal`) from a transverse-field ground state toward that of the programmed problem. The problem is static: biases are held on chip, the answer latched as bits. Table 8.3's bet, special-purpose scale now, is a trade: count and connectivity for gates, logical qubits and any route to fault tolerance.

**What it needs.** The qubits, the long couplers' multilayer routing (`cx_lr`) and the SFQ flux DACs (`ct_fluxdac`) all *require* one superconducting process (`fab_sc`). Readout (`ro_fluxro`) *requires* the qubits and the DACs: a quantum flux parametron latches each qubit's current, on-chip shift registers carry the bit out. Static biases let DACs control "tens of thousands of qubits and couplers with just 200 bias wires" [C][A8]. The empty layers mean: no encoding, so a qubit is a Hamiltonian term, not a memory; no readout during the anneal, so no syndrome for a code or decoder. Errors are never detected, only averaged over samples: more qubits buy larger problems, not better answers.

**Where it stands.** Three machines, two devices, 14 of 32 cells documented (Table 8.1), no gate. Advantage2 (D-Wave, May 2025; 4,400+ qubits, Zephyr 20-way, 12.5 kW [C][A1]) supersedes Advantage (2020). D-Wave's "beyond-classical" spin-glass result (Science, March 2025) was substantially eroded within weeks by tensor-network and variational methods [D][A2]; D-Wave maintains its largest instances remain unmatched [P][A3]. Qilimanjaro's coherent annealer shows no device, count or benchmark. §2.8 scores only E 4 and F 2; H1-2026 revenue fell 67% [C][A4]; a CHIPS agreement (up to $100 M, 8 September 2026) funds work toward a 100,000-qubit annealer and a gate-model system [C][R][B19].

**Exceptions and borrowings.** No cell falls off the slots; all fit here best. Both Advantage machines list dispersive readout (`ro_disp`), which the map says *replaces* the flux latch, as an alternate: perimeter resonators read the shift-register bits after the latch, so the two sit in series: a hybrid, not rivals. Qilimanjaro's coupler and DAC cells are inferred, its readout undisclosed: a data gap. Borrowings run outward: the D-Wave gate-model dual-rail system takes the flux DACs as an alternate, and IBM Quantum Loon and its successors use the long couplers for qLDPC checks.

**The next test.** §5.2 has no annealing row; D-Wave's roadmap serves. Its 20,000-qubit system for 2029 and 100,000 qubits by 2031 [R][A4] rest on "superconducting interconnect technologies" for "multi-chip fabrics" [C][A4], the layer the architecture leaves empty. Confirmation by March 2028: a multi-chip anneal with couplers across chip boundaries, or a beyond-classical claim surviving a year of classical challenge. Demotion: neither by then, and Advantage3 moved or dropped.

**Machines of this architecture (register: 3, of them 2 devices, 0 gate-capable).** **D-Wave Quantum** — Advantage (deployed, 5,000), Advantage2 (deployed, 4,400); **Qilimanjaro Quantum Tech** — coherent annealer (planned).

*Cells outside the architecture's slots (3).* coherent annealer: L4 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; coherent annealer: L5 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station; coherent annealer: L6 undisclosed where the architecture expects a station.

### 8.4 Trends — eight hypotheses tested on the register

Each hypothesis is a falsifiable statement about the population; the test is the computation named with it, run by the build on the register's current rows; the verdict is *supported*, *partly supported* or *not supported*, with the sample size. What each verdict rests on, and what would overturn it, is in §8.7.

**H1 — Physical qubit count has moved to the atoms; superconducting machines no longer compete on count.**

*Test.* Median physical qubits per family over all devices and over gate-capable devices (89 of 110; analog simulators, arrays without an entangling gate and single-qubit testbeds excluded); the largest gate-capable device per family; cohort medians for the two largest families.

| Family | Devices: median qubits (n) | Gate-capable: median qubits (n) | Largest gate-capable device | Largest device of any kind |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| neutral atoms | 256 (11) | 140 (7) | Phoenix (Gen-1) and Gen-2 — 1,180 | 6,100-qubit tweezer array — 6,100 |
| superconducting circuits | 59 (62) | 72 (53) | IBM Quantum Osprey — 433 | IBM Quantum Osprey — 433 |
| trapped ions | 30 (15) | 30 (14) | Helios — 98 | 2-D site-resolved ion simulator — 512 |
| photonics | 12 (4) | 12 (3) | Lucy — 12 | Lucy — 12 |
| semiconductor spins | 11 (5) | 11 (5) | HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots) — 18 | HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots) — 18 |

*Result.* The atoms lead by ×4.3 on all devices and ×1.9 on gate-capable ones. Cohort medians (gate-capable; n in brackets) — superconducting circuits: 2021 40 (6), 2022 52.5 (4), 2023 108.5 (4), 2024 94.5 (14), 2025 66 (18), 2026 108 (7); neutral atoms: 2023 1,180 (1), 2024 140 (1), 2025 180 (4), 2026 20 (1). *Verdict:* **supported**. The atoms' lead in count is real among processors and much larger among trap arrays: the thousands-of-qubits machines are arrays that do not yet run an entangling gate, while the largest gate-capable tweezer processor and the largest transmon lattice are within a factor of a few of each other. The cohort medians move with who enters the register in a given year (small first chips from new entrants) more than with the leaders, and are too thin per cohort to carry a trend on their own.

**H2 — Two-qubit error is converging across the three large families at the median, while the best single number stays with the ions.**

*Test.* Median and best `err_2q_median` over devices per family, excluding hero-pair numbers, targets and component demonstrations. *Result.* superconducting circuits median 4.2×10⁻³, best 1.0×10⁻³ (Aqumen Seeker), n = 24; trapped ions median 4.0×10⁻³, best 7.9×10⁻⁴ (Helios), n = 7; neutral atoms median 4.0×10⁻³, best 1.6×10⁻³ (448-atom fault-tolerant processor), n = 5; photonics median 6.2×10⁻², best 6.2×10⁻² (Belenos), n = 2; semiconductor spins median 3.6×10⁻³, best 3.0×10⁻³ (HRL exchange-only silicon spin processor (18 EO qubits from 54 quantum dots)), n = 3. The spread of the three large medians is ×1.1. *Verdict:* **supported**. A convergence of medians with a persistent gap at the best is what one expects when the median is set by the many second-tier machines and the best by a few labs that have run the same platform for a decade. The verdict describes one snapshot of the register; whether the medians keep converging is a question for the next edition's cohorts, not for this one.
