The families separate along the qubit axis more than along the error axis — the reading behind H1 and H2; hover a mark for the machine.</figcaption></figure>

**H3 — Control stays external: integrated control is a minority confined to spins, annealers and a few traps; no superconducting device of 100 qubits or more is driven from inside the cryostat.**

*Test.* The register's `control_placement` classified as room-temperature electronics, optics, integrated (on-chip microwave, cryo-CMOS, SFQ, flux DACs, 4 K) or undisclosed; the integrated class by family; superconducting devices ≥ 100 qubits with cryogenic control that is not a demo. *Result.* External 125 of 160; integrated 22 (superconducting circuits 6, trapped ions 7, semiconductor spins 6, quantum annealers 3), of which cryogenic 15; superconducting devices ≥ 100 qubits with non-demo cryogenic control: 0. *Verdict:* **supported**. The attribute the map treats as the slow-moving one (§7.2: control placement) moves slowly in the register too: the integrated class is where the physics forces it — flux DACs on annealers, CMOS next to CMOS spins, microwave electrodes in surface traps — and a plan elsewhere.

**H4 — Running a code on the hardware is becoming the entry ticket: the share of new machines that have done so rises by cohort, and closed-loop decoding follows.**

*Test.* Machines with at least one code in the register's code table, by cohort 2023–2026; machines whose decoding is in the loop. *Result.* 48 of 160 machines list a code — 35 of them devices that have run it, the rest announced or planned machines whose roadmap names one (superconducting circuits 29, trapped ions 9, neutral atoms 6, photonics 2, semiconductor spins 1, defect spins 1); share by cohort 2023 27 % (3/11), 2024 22 % (6/27), 2025 34 % (17/50), 2026 26 % (12/46); decoding in the loop: 5 (Aurora, Rigetti Ankaa-2, System Model H1 (H1-1), System Model H2 (H2-1, H2-2), Willow). *Verdict:* **partly supported**. The share rises but not monotonically, and the loop is closed on a handful of machines: the entry ticket is a code *demonstration*, not yet a decoder in the cycle.

**H5 — Modularity is a roadmap, not a machine: a link between modules is the rarest thing in the register.**

*Test.* Primary interconnect cells (Table 8.2): machines with no interconnect at all (`none`), with packaging or fan-out inside one module but no link, with an undisclosed link, with a link the map has no station for, and with a link station between modules. *Result.* No interconnect 122 of 160 (76 %); in-module packaging or fan-out only, 15; undisclosed, 6; an Atlas gap, 1; a link station between modules on 16 machines (superconducting circuits 10, trapped ions 3, photonics 2, defect spins 1) — 144 of 160 (90 %) have no link between modules. The layer with the most `none` cells is Interconnect (122). *Verdict:* **supported**. This is a snapshot of the register, not a trend: every roadmap that reaches thousands of qubits assumes a link between modules; the register holds one on 16 machines, 6 of them devices — the rest announced or planned. The layer where the architecture must go is the layer where it has least been.

**H6 — Roadmaps fall short on the error budget, not on the qubit count.**

*Test.* The register's roadmap-feasibility table (16 roadmaps × the target algorithms): verdicts, and among the SHORT verdicts the deficit's nature. *Result.* FEASIBLE 23, NOT EVALUABLE 40, SHORT 65; of the 65 SHORT verdicts 56 carry an error or gate-budget deficit and 9 a qubit-only deficit. *Verdict:* **supported**. Qubit counts are the roadmaps' own currency and they budget it generously; the logical error per operation and the gate count of the target algorithm are where the same roadmaps miss by one to six orders of magnitude.

**H7 — The announcement gap widens: the newest cohort is increasingly announcements and plans rather than devices.**

*Test.* Share of announced or planned rows per cohort 2023–2026; retirements by family. *Result.* 2023 9 % (1/11), 2024 7 % (2/27), 2025 14 % (7/50), 2026 52 % (24/46); retired: superconducting circuits 6. *Verdict:* **supported** — with the caveat that the newest cohort is a partial year and some of its announcements will become devices; the shape to watch is whether the share falls back as the year closes. "Widens" is read off the cohorts of one register snapshot, not off successive editions; the trend claim is tested when the 2026 cohort closes.

**H8 — The families' ideas converge: a growing set of stations is shared across families.**

*Test.* Stations occupied in the primary role, counted by the number of families that occupy them. *Result.* 100 stations occupied; 21 by two families or more, 4 by three or more (Static nearest-neighbour lattice `cx_nn` — 4, MWPM / Sparse Blossom (+correlated matching) `dec_mwpm` — 4, Non-local qLDPC codes (bivariate-bicycle, 'gross') `code_qldpc` — 3, Baseband electrical control (spins, Majorana) `ct_base` — 3). *Verdict:* **not supported**. The families keep their own toolboxes; what they share is a *role* — mid-circuit readout, erasure conversion, a link between modules — filled by a different station on each architecture. Convergence, where it exists, is at the level of the map's attributes (§7), not of its stations — and "growing" is a hypothesis about editions, not a measurement on this snapshot, which gives one count.

### 8.5 Forecasts — what the register predicts, and how the next edition will score them

A hypothesis says what the register shows; a forecast predicts what the next register will show. Each forecast below names its indicator, its value today, the value expected at the next two editions (2027.09 and 2028.09), the basis — the historical series, the register's own announcements, or both — and a confidence; the build writes the same rows to `data/forecast-ledger.json`, and the edition that reaches the date prints the outcome next to the forecast. Two limits govern the confidence: the series are short (five to eight yearly points) and dated by press releases, so the forecasts are directional with ranges rather than point estimates; and announcements are discounted — the register's three named rows that missed their date (IBM Quantum Condor, IBM Quantum Kookaburra, IQM Halocene) are the reminder that an announced machine is a forecast, not a device.

**Table 8.5 — the forecast ledger (scored by the 2027.09 and 2028.09 editions)**

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| F1b | largest gate-capable neutral-atom device | 1,180 (flat since 2023) | 1,180–1,300: one announced candidate above the frontier within the year (see basis); even odds it lands | 1,300–3,000: the 10,000-qubit plans sit at 2028 and are discounted to a third of their count on the register's delivery record | frontier series 2023 1,180, 2024 1,180, 2025 1,180, 2026 1,180; announced above the frontier: Magne — 1,225 (2026); Libra — 10,000 (2028) | medium | a gate-capable tweezer processor above 3,000 qubits by 2028.09, or none above 1,180 by then |
| F1c | largest gate-capable trapped-ion device | 98 (2026) | 120–10000: the historical ×1.30/yr gives ≈128 by 2027; the largest announced system (10000) caps the range | 200–400 | frontier series 2020 20, 2021 20, 2022 32, 2023 64, 2024 64, 2025 98, 2026 98 — the only family whose frontier still grows year on year; announced: Tempo — 100 (2025); Superion 256 — 256 (2026); NFQC-1k — 1,000 (2026); IonQ Superion 10K — 10,000 (2026) | medium | a trapped-ion device with entangling gates on more than 260 qubits by 2027.09, or the frontier still at 98 |

| F5 | machines with integrated control; superconducting devices ≥ 100 qubits driven cryogenically | 22 / 0 | ≤ 25 / 0: the candidates are demos and claims (Google fluxonium line (ex-Atlantic Quantum), IBM Quantum Heron r2, OQC GENESIS, OQC Toshiko, QuamCore, QuantWare VIO-40K, foundry 8-qubit array); IBM's cryo-CMOS is scheduled with Starling (2029) | ≤ 30 / 0–1 | H3's mechanism: integration happens where physics forces it (spins, annealers, surface traps) and is a plan elsewhere; the register's control-placement field lists the plans | high | a ≥ 100-qubit superconducting device with production cryogenic control before 2028.09 |
| F6 | roadmap checkpoints the register can score by 2028 | 4 roadmaps dated 2025–2028: nordquantique-2029 (2028-2029), oqc-titan (2028), psiquantum-2033 (2028-2033), quera-phase3 (2026-2028) | the checkpoints dated 2026–2027 are scored; on H6's distribution (56 of 65 SHORT verdicts on error or gates) the expectation is that they miss on the error budget, not on qubits | the 2028 checkpoints are scored; at least two of three miss on the logical error per operation | the register's roadmap-feasibility verdicts and their dates | medium | a dated checkpoint met on qubits, error and gate budget together |

Reading the ledger. The reliable part is the shape, not the digits: the count frontier of the two largest families has not moved for three and four years while the announced frontier runs a decade ahead of it (F1), the error frontier improves by a steady third to a half per year and the medians follow it with a lag of about three years (F2), and the adoption curves — codes on hardware, links, integrated control — are set by the delivery of the machines the register already lists as planned, discounted by how often such rows have slipped (F3–F5). What the ledger cannot do is time a single machine; what it can do is be wrong in public, one edition later.

### 8.6 Where the architecture is going

Of the eight hypotheses, 6 are supported, 1 partly and 1 not. Read together they draw one direction rather than eight. **From count to encoded qubits and links.** The two least populated layers — code/decoder and interconnect — are exactly the two every roadmap depends on (H4, H5, H6): the next generation of machines will be judged by the logical error per cycle and by the link between modules, and the register already shows the count race losing its meaning (H1). **A division of roles between families rather than a winner.** The largest devices are tweezer arrays, the best gate is an ion trap, the fastest clock a transmon lattice and the room-temperature fabrication is photonic; the medians converge (H2) while the extremes stay apart, and the stations do not cross the family lines (H8) — the architecture that reaches the goals of §4 will more likely be a *composition* of architectures joined by links than a single architecture grown large. **The slow variable is control.** Integrated control is a plan for the families that can afford room-temperature racks and a necessity only where the physics forces it (H3); the map's placement attribute will move last. **The binding constraint is the error budget.** Roadmaps are short by orders of magnitude on error and gate count and rarely on qubits (H6), so the machines that matter next are the ones that move the logical error per operation, whatever their qubit count.

### 8.7 Verification and limits

**Table 8.7 — what each verdict rests on**

| Hypothesis | Verdict | Sample | Filters applied | Evidence grade of the inputs | What would overturn it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| H1 | **supported** | 89 gate-capable devices | status ∈ {deployed, demonstrated, retired}; no target/component/analog/no-gate flag | register field `physical_qubits_num` (press-level for most machines) | a superconducting device cohort whose median passes the atoms |
| H2 | **supported** | superconducting circuits 24, trapped ions 7, neutral atoms 5, photonics 2, semiconductor spins 3 | devices; hero-pair, target and component numbers excluded | register field `err_2q_median` (paper-level where a figure is cited, else press) | a family median more than ×2 from the others, or a non-ion best |
| H3 | **supported** | 160 | none (all rows); the ≥ 100-qubit clause on devices | control layer cells: 47 % ✅ (75/160) | integrated control on more than 30 % of machines, or a ≥ 100-qubit superconducting device driven cryogenically |
| H4 | **partly supported** | 134 machines in cohorts 2023–2026 | cohort = year of the status date | code layer cells: 34 % ✅ (54/160) | a falling share across three cohorts, or ten machines with a decoder in the loop |
| H5 | **supported** | 160 | primary cells only; none, undisclosed, gap and in-module packaging counted as “no link” | interconnect layer cells: 25 % ✅ (40/160) | links between modules on a quarter of the machines |
| H6 | **supported** | 128 roadmap × algorithm rows | verdict = SHORT | register table `roadmap-feasibility` (targets as published by the vendors) | qubit-only deficits above half of the SHORT verdicts |
| H7 | **supported** | 134 machines in cohorts 2023–2026 | status ∈ {announced, planned} | register field `status` (press-level by nature) | the newest cohort’s share falling to the previous cohorts’ level |
| H8 | **not supported** | 100 occupied stations | primary role; none, undisclosed and gap cells excluded | all primary cells: 44 % ✅ | half of the occupied stations shared by two families or more |

Limits. The cohort is the year of the register's status date, which mixes first light, general availability and the latest milestone; the classifiers are string rules over free-text fields, printed at the top of the chapter's generator in the map's repository (`build/machines_chapter.py`) so that a reviewer can object to a rule rather than to a number; the population is the register's, which favours machines with an English-language document. 21 machines have no verified cell at all (press pages only) and enter the counts at press level: 50-qubit ion quantum computer (Rosatom roadmap), 72-qubit neutral-atom (Rb) quantum computer prototype (50 qubits in Dec 2024), Alice & Bob Boson 4, Alloy Pioneer (Quobly), Fujitsu 1,000-qubit machine, Groove Quantum (no named machine), Haituo (Quafu platform), Hanyuan-1, Hanyuan-2, Hitachi x Intel 18A spin programme, IBM Quantum System One, Ehningen, IBM Quantum System Two at Amaravati Quantum Valley Tech Park, IBM Quantum System Two, Donostia-San Sebastian, KRISS 50-qubit-class superconducting system, KRISS domestic superconducting QPU, Lucy, Qinghe-1, Quantum Spain quantum computer at BSC, Quantum X Labs neutral-atom platform (50+ qubits, claimed), Rigetti Ankaa-3, Tianyan-504 / "Xiaohong" chip. Every hypothesis is re-tested by each build; a verdict that flips between editions is itself a finding and will be reported in the changelog.
